Academic libraries are trying a variety’ of strategies of
varying sophistication to address this need. Our effort has
focused on analyzing and then emulating the techniques
most commonly used by reference librarians when helping
library users identify databases’ to search, We developed a
database selection algorithm, which mimics the database
selection strategy used by reference librarians (Ma, 2000).
We gathered detailed characterization data describing each
of 146 databases currently available to the University’ of
Illinois at Urbana—Champaign (UIUC) Library. These data
were put into a SQL database, We acquired database-con
trolled vocabularies and enter into this SQL database to
further characterize 82 of the 146 databases. Due to time
constraints, we were not able to get the. controlled vocabu
lary files for all of the databases included, 46 of the 82
databases had full controlled vocabulary files from database
producers. The remaining 36 of 82 databases contained only
5—IOU;’ of data-mining subject terms derived from 1998’s
database records) (Ma, 2000), We developed a prototype
Web-based electronic database selection expert system,
named Smart Database Selector (the Selector), in 1999 (Ma,
2000), The target users of the Selector are general library
users and library public service staff who assist users for
their information needs.
The query interface includes’ three search for as fsee
Fig. I):
Form 1 is’ the search form for searching fre.e-text key
words and phrases with Boolean operators. The free-text
keywords can he word(s) or phrase(s) from .he topic, can he
subject term , names (last name first), geographic areas, etc
This search form also automaticall handles righ.t-hand
truncations (e.g., acre engin will return databases that in.de.x.
aerospace eng.ineering,” “aeronautical e.ngineering,” “aero
space engineers,” etc.).
From 2’. allows a us:er to choose a subject term, if he/s:he
has difficu.lty e.xpressing the topic in fre.e-text terms. Th.e
Sele.ctor will display a list of databases re.le.vart to the
yelecte,d subject term.

as listed in Figures 3 and 4.
After completing initial demographic background ques
tionnaires, subjects were asked to pick a topic of their own
choosing and use the Selector to suggest databases. By so
doing we gave the subjects the opportunity to get familiar
with the Selector. Then, they were asked to use the Selector
to identify electronic resources for the predefined search
questions. The entire test process was carefully observed. At
the end of the test session all individuals were asked to
complete a Post-Test Que%tionnaire proi iding qualitati’ e
kedback on the usahilit> of the interfltce. Before leaving, a
brief interview was t pically cnnducted—flnr c’iample. ask
ing if %helbe felt the Selector was useful, if heashe liked the
Selector, if heishe had suggestions tiw ilnproements. etc.
Transaction logs were kept of all searches done 1”> r.uI!ject%
during the testing. Full details regarding search arguments
suhniitted, search limits applied, and results retrieved (i.e..
lists of database names) were recorded in transaction logs
br later analysis.

ence ie.g.. as a lihrnr graduate assistant I. Because of the
budget constraints, we were only able to recruit 22 subjects.
Search scenarios for testing were selected from real
reference questions collected at the Librar> ‘s central refer
ence desk, with an intention to represent a variety of subject
areas. To find out if the system was useful for library users
and for library professionals as well, we decided to give
library user group and library staff group different sets of
search scenarios to pertbrm. The Library staff group’s
search scenarios included a 1w tougher reftrence questions
and questions that had more conditions. Figures 3 and 4
show the search scenarios used in this ucabilit test.
